| DOC-A.1-72 | **FILE-CONTROL entry, ASSIGN clause — consistency rules for external file connectors**, §12.4.5 GR1, required + documented | §12.4.5.3 GR1 b) requires the file control entries that reference one external file connector to have *"A consistent specification for data-name-1, device-name-1, and literal-1 in the ASSIGN clause"* and leaves the rules to the implementor. **COBOL.NET's rule: the entries must name the same file target and the same data-name-1, and nothing more.** (1) **The TO phrase is compared by the operand that names the file** (item 71), not by every word written: a leading device class is dropped, a literal is compared by its value and a device-name by its spelling. So `ASSIGN TO DISK "xa.dat"`, `ASSIGN TO PRINTER "xa.dat"` and `ASSIGN TO "xa.dat"` are consistent, and a device-name and a literal with the same spelling are the same name, so `ASSIGN TO XADAT` and `ASSIGN TO "xadat"` are consistent. Item 71's host-path mapping is NOT applied before the comparison: `ASSIGN TO XADAT` and `ASSIGN TO "xadat.txt"` are NOT consistent, although both open the host file `xadat.txt`. The comparison ignores case, so `"Data.txt"` and `"data.txt"` are consistent, even though on a case-sensitive host they name different physical files; the file opened is the one named in the program that executes the OPEN. An entry with a TO phrase and one without are not consistent. (2) **The USING data-name-1 is compared by spelling, qualifiers included, ignoring case.** `IN` and `OF` are the same, so `FNAME IN G` and `FNAME OF G` are consistent; `FNAME` and `FNAME OF G` are not, and neither are an entry with a USING phrase and one without. The check runs only when checking for EC-EXTERNAL-FILE-MISMATCH is enabled. It happens when a second program that describes the external file is activated: the two ASSIGN fields are part of the `SelectFingerprint` each program registers with `ExternalStore.Describe` (built in `OoEmitter.EmitExternalDescribes`), and a difference raises **EC-EXTERNAL-FILE-MISMATCH** (§14.8.4.4), which is fatal. **⛔ The two programs still hold SEPARATE STORAGE for an identically-spelled data-name-1, and that is deliberate.** §12.4.5.3 GR3 b) associates the connector with the file named *"in the runtime element that executes the OPEN, SORT, or MERGE statement"*, which only makes sense if the entries MAY name different data items. GR1 i) shows how the standard words it when it wants the opposite (FILE STATUS: *"where data-name-4 shall reference the same corresponding external data item"*), and §13.4.5.4 GR2 c) 2. does the same for LINAGE; GR1 b) says neither. Implemented at `FileConnector.Associate`, called with the executing program's own rendered specification (kb/Work PB673). | — |
| DOC-A.1-73 | **FILE-CONTROL entry, ASSIGN clause — USING phrase meaning and rules**, §12.4.5 GR4, required + documented | **The content of data-name-1, with leading and trailing SPACES removed, IS the assign target, and it is then mapped to a host path by exactly the rule that maps literal-1** (`CobolFile.ResolveHostPath`: a target already carrying a `.`, `/` or `\` is used verbatim; any other is lowercased and given a `.txt` extension in the current directory). So `PIC X(20) VALUE "  data/in.dat  "` names `data/in.dat`, and `VALUE "CUSTMAST"` names `custmast.txt`. **The removal of spaces is the determination that makes the feature usable at all** — data-name-1 is an alphanumeric item, so its content is space-padded to its declared size, and an unstripped name would ask the operating environment for a file whose name ends in blanks. **The association is made at every OPEN, SORT and MERGE and at no other statement** (§12.4.5.3 GR3's own timing sentence): one connector reaches different physical files during a run unit (§9.1.21), and a DELETE FILE before the first of those three finds no associated file and takes §14.9.10.4 GR14's successful '05'. **Content that is empty after the space removal, or that carries a character below U+0020, names no physical file**: the association cannot be made, so the statement is unsuccessful with §9.1.13.6 item 2's '31' — the status the standard reserves for exactly this failure. Everything the operating environment rejects beyond that is an ordinary open failure ('30'/'35'/'37'), never an escaping exception. Implemented at `FileConnector.Associate`, the ONE writer of a connector's host path; §12.4.5.2 SR7's own limits on data-name-1 (alphanumeric, not subordinate to the file's FD) are compile-time, `COBOLNET1810`/`COBOLNET1811`. (kb/Work PB324) | `conformance:2023/pb324_assign_using_dynamic`; `conformance:85/pb324_assign_using_85` |
| DOC-A.1-10 | **ASSIGN clause, USING phrase — consistency rules between data-name-1's content and device-name-1 / literal-1**, §12.4.5 GR4, optional + documented if provided | **There are NONE.** §12.4.5.3 GR4's second sentence asks for "the consistency rules, **if any**, that apply between the content of the data item referenced by data-name-1 and either the specification of device-name-1 or the value of literal-1", and "if any" makes the empty set a complete answer. COBOL.NET requires no relationship whatever: where a file control entry writes BOTH phrases — `ASSIGN TO "pb324mix.dat" USING WS-NAME` — GR3 b) applies "when the USING phrase of the ASSIGN clause is specified" with no condition on the TO phrase, so the content wins outright, literal-1 is never used to identify a file, and an entirely unrelated content is accepted rather than diagnosed. **The consequence, stated so nobody re-derives it as a defect:** the inconsistency branch of §12.4.5.3 GR3's closing sentence is unreachable BY DEFINITION, and the '31' this implementation does report comes from the CONTENT rule of item 73 (empty, or a control character) rather than from any comparison with device-name-1/literal-1. There is likewise no device-name-1 vocabulary to be consistent with: the TO phrase's operand is a file-name mapping (item 71), not a device. | `conformance:2023/pb324_assign_using_dynamic` |
| DOC-A.1-129 | **OPEN statement — validation of fixed file attributes**, §14.9.27.4 GR10, required + documented | **The validated set is exactly what each organization's PHYSICAL FORMAT ENCODES.** ⛔ **Owner decision 2026-09-07 (question 24), verbatim: _"Let's match GNUCobol's implementation in spirit. No sidecar of any type."_** (kb/Work PB802) — it REPLACED the `.cbattr` catalog sidecar this determination previously named, and NTFS alternate data streams were considered and rejected with it. COBOL.NET writes and reads NOTHING beside a data file, so a physical file's fixed file attributes are whatever ITS OWN BYTES record. GR10 grants exactly that latitude: "The implementor defines which of the fixed-file attributes are validated during the execution of the OPEN statement. The validation of fixed-file attributes may vary depending on the organization or storage medium of the file." **Three organizations, three answers, each derived from that one rule rather than listed.** **(1) Record sequential with FIXED-length records, and line sequential — NOTHING is validated.** §9.1.7.2: "In record sequential files the length of each record is determined by any information the implementor may add to the record on the physical storage medium (such as record length headers)" — COBOL.NET adds none to a fixed-length one, which is plain bytes — and "In line sequential files the length of each record is determined by the number of characters between the preceding line delimiter and the following line delimiter or the end of file if no line delimiter is present", where the delimiters encode no attribute either. The standard answers every disagreement such a re-read can produce with a SUCCESSFUL completion rather than a refused OPEN: §9.1.13.2 item 3's '04' ("A READ statement is successfully executed but the physical record from the file is shorter than or longer than the minimum or maximum length of records allowed for the fixed file attributes for that file"), item 5's '06' and item 7's '09'. Writing a print, report or extract file and reading it back under a different record description is exactly the idiom those three statuses exist for — measured, a validated record size broke six conforming programs on this repository's own corpus, one into an infinite READ loop — so a '39' there would reject legal source. `RecordLayoutNotice` remains the stderr notice for the arithmetic case and leaves the I-O status alone. **(2) Record sequential with RECORD VARYING — the record-length header must PARSE, and nothing more.** §9.1.7.2's "such as record length headers" is precisely what this processor adds here: a 4-byte little-endian length prefix per record (`RecordFraming`), whose method §12.4.5.11.4 GR5 grants ("If the RECORD DELIMITER clause is not specified, the method used for determining the length of a variable-length record is specified by the implementor") and whose GR1 it obeys ("Any method used shall not be reflected in the record area or the record size used within the function, method, or program"). A first prefix naming more bytes than the file holds is not a framed file at all, so the connector's declared VARIABLE **record type** — a §9.1.6 fixed file attribute — contradicts the medium and the OPEN is unsuccessful with '39'. A prefix that parses but falls outside the RECORD clause's bounds is NOT a conflict: that is item 3's '04' on the READ, which a '39' at the OPEN would make unreachable. A file shorter than one prefix states nothing and is not a conflict. **(3) RELATIVE and INDEXED — whatever the framed store's HEADER carries**: the **organization**, the **record type** (fixed or variable), the **minimum and maximum logical record size in bytes**, and the **key table** — each key's byte window, DUPLICATES phrase, SUPPRESS WHEN value and collating-sequence fingerprint (the sequence identified by its weights, not by the alphabet-name that produced them). Those two organizations live in an implementor-defined store whose STRUCTURE is those attributes, and the header is where that structure is written down — IN THE FILE, as GnuCOBOL's ISAM key definitions are in the file. A store whose header is absent or unreadable is not a store this build can interpret: '39', loud, rather than a silent misread. A ZERO-BYTE file is the one tolerated exception — it holds no records, so it can misread nothing, and it states no attribute to contradict. ⛔ **THE KEY CHECK HAS A DOCUMENTED RUNTIME OPT-OUT — `COBOLNET_KEYCHECK=OFF`**, GnuCOBOL's `COB_KEYCHECK=OFF` under this repository's own naming (`COBOLNET_`, never `COBOL_`, which is the external-switch family's prefix). It is read ONCE per run unit, in `FileRegistry`'s constructor, and is registered in `RuntimeConfig` like every other variable the runtime honours. It is part of this determination rather than an escape from it: with it OFF the key table leaves the validated set for that run and the organization, record type and record sizes stay in it. ⚖ **Surveyed, not assumed** (the standing latitude rule): GnuCOBOL's own testsuite shows exactly two status-39 cases — `variable-length SEQUENTIAL data integrity` (a RECORD VARYING FD over a file whose record-length header does not parse → `OPEN ERROR: 39`) and `INDEXED undeclared keys` (three FDs on one physical file declaring three, two and one keys; the one whose key table disagrees answers '39', and `COB_KEYCHECK=OFF` opens it) — which is the behaviour reproduced above, organization for organization. ⚠ **This REPLACED a catalog, and the change is owner-visible in two ways.** The framed store's new header is a FORMAT CHANGE: a relative or indexed file written by an earlier build carries no header and is refused with '39' rather than silently misread. And kb/Work PB193's original reproduction — a RELATIVE file reopened INPUT through a SEQUENTIAL or LINE SEQUENTIAL FD — no longer answers '39' but '00', because the sequential organization's format encodes nothing for a relative store to contradict; the reverse direction (a plain sequential file opened through a RELATIVE or INDEXED FD) still answers '39', and `RecordLayoutNotice` still reports the byte-count disagreement on stderr. ⛔ **The indexed half is not only latitude:** §12.4.5.12.4 GR3 requires a prime key's data description and relative location to "be the same as that used when the file was created", and §12.4.5.6.4 GR3 says the same of every alternate key and of the number of alternate keys ("shall be the same as that used when the physical file was created" — the two rules are worded differently and the difference is quoted here rather than smoothed over). Neither states a consequence where it is written; GR10's conflict condition is the mechanism that detects a violation, so carrying the key table in the store header is what gives those two rules an effect at all. The recorded descriptor is the key's window plus its collating sequence, which is the whole of "the data description … as well as their relative location" this processor can act on: §12.4.5.12.3 SR2 confines a record key to category alphanumeric or category national, §12.4.5.12.4 GR1 makes key equality a relation condition under the file's collating sequence, and both native sequences are one code-unit ordinal over the UTF-16 substrate (items 33 and 188) — so two descriptions sharing a window and a sequence order every key value identically whatever their category. ⛔ **This set is NOT empty, and deliberately unlike item 50's.** DELETE FILE destroys the file, so validating nothing costs the program nothing; an OPEN is the opposite — a relative or indexed store's structure is read back *through* the connector's description. **The three §9.1.6 attributes outside the set for every organization.** The **code set** is a DETERMINATION: §13.18.13.4 GR7 makes it the native character set when no CODE-SET clause is written and GR2/GR6 the named alphabet's when one is, and since kb/Work PB793 those two CAN differ (the `EBCDIC` code-name is a real alternate device code set, §2 row 27). It is not validated for two reasons GR10's latitude exists to accommodate: the whole purpose of a CODE-SET clause is to read a physical file some OTHER system wrote, which carries no COBOL.NET header at all, so the check would fire only where it is not wanted; and reading a converted file through a description with NO CODE-SET clause is GR7's own default and the raw-medium idiom a dump or transcode utility is written in (what golden `2023/pb793_code_set_ebcdic` uses to prove the conversion happened at all), so a '39' there would reject legal source. The **minimum and maximum physical record size** is outside the set because the managed I-O model does no blocking and BLOCK CONTAINS is accepted inert under A.3 item 5. The **record delimiter** is outside it because RECORD DELIMITER as a clause has no compiler surface, and the delimiter distinction that IS realized — §9.1.7.2's record-sequential vs line-sequential — is a SEQUENTIAL-organization attribute whose format records nothing; §9.1.13.2 item 5's '06' is the standard's answer to a delimiter disagreement. **§9.1.6's own last-sentence obligation** — "The implementor shall specify whether the ability to share a physical file is a fixed file attribute" — is answered **NO**: a sharing mode is established per FILE CONNECTOR by the SHARING clause or the OPEN's SHARING phrase (§14.9.27.4 GR21–GR23) and arbitrated live against the connectors currently open (§9.1.15 Table 19), so it is not a property the physical file carries between run units. **Where it is implemented:** `FileConnector.Open` asks ONE virtual, `FileConnector.FixedAttributeConflict`, before `OpenCore` (GR25 leaves an unsuccessful OPEN's file unaffected); its three overrides are the three answers above — the base "this format encodes nothing", `SequentialConnector`'s varying-prefix parse, and `KeyedConnector`'s store header compared through `FixedFileAttributes.Conflicts`, the one place the comparison is written. `RecordFraming` owns the store header's bytes because the header IS the format. | `conformance:85/open_fixed_attribute_conflict; conformance:2023/open_fixed_attribute_conflict_ix; unit:FixedFileAttributeFormatTests.EveryOrganizationStatesWhatItsOwnFormatEncodes; unit:FixedFileAttributeFormatTests.TheStoreHeaderIsTheONLYArtifact_AndItIsInsideTheDataFile; unit:FixedFileAttributeFormatTests.TheKeyCheckOptOutOpensTheSameFile_AndNarrowsNothingElse` |
| DOC-A.1-90 | **Function-identifier — the result when argument rules are violated and checking for EC-ARGUMENT-FUNCTION is not enabled**, §15.3 rule 14, required — A.1 says “The value returned does not have to be documented in the implementor’s user documentation”, and it is documented here anyway: three different values were in play across the corpus and a golden may not pin an unwritten determination | §15.3 rule 14: “If the EC-ARGUMENT-FUNCTION exception condition is set to exist and checking for EC-ARGUMENT-FUNCTION is not enabled, the implementor defines the result of the function reference.” COBOL.NET’s substituted result is **the zero value of the type the function returns**, one rule for every intrinsic. A **numeric** result is **0** — `ANNUITY`, `PRESENT-VALUE`, `RANDOM`, `FACTORIAL`, `LOG`/`LOG10`, `MOD`/`REM` with a zero divisor, the `INTEGER-OF-*` / `*-TO-YYYY*` date family, `ORD` on an empty argument. An **alphanumeric or national** result is **spaces**: for the fixed one-character returns `CHAR` and `CHAR-NATIONAL` that is **one space** (the `OVER`/`ZERO`/`WRAP` legs of the two CHAR-NATIONAL goldens each read ORD 33 — the space — rather than the in-range character). A **boolean** result is the **zero value of the boolean item the function returns** — for `BOOLEAN-OF-INTEGER` with a rejected argument-1 and a valid argument-2, argument-2 zero boolean positions (§15.13.4 r1 sizes the returned item from argument-2). And where the returned LENGTH is itself derived from the rejected argument — `FORMATTED-DATE`, `FORMATTED-DATETIME`, `FORMATTED-TIME`, `BASECONVERT`, and `BOOLEAN-OF-INTEGER` when it is **argument-2 itself** that is rejected (§15.13.3 r2 — argument-2 *is* the returned length, so no length survives the rejection) — it is a **zero-length value**, which a receiving item pads to spaces. And where the returned length is fixed by neither the function nor the rejected argument but by something that SURVIVES the rejection, the general clause governs and the answer is spaces: for `LOCALE-DATE`, `LOCALE-TIME` and `LOCALE-TIME-FROM-SECONDS` §15.52.4 / §15.53.4 / §15.54.4 rule 3 each say “The length of the returned value depends on the format indicated in the locale” — rejecting argument-1 leaves the locale, and so the length, entirely intact — and the answer is **one alphanumeric space**. One rather than the width the locale would have produced, because DETERMINATION L10 renders `d_fmt` / `t_fmt` as the culture’s own patterns, whose width varies with the VALUE as well as with the format (under `en-US`’s `M/d/yyyy` a date is 8 or 10 positions wide), so once the value is rejected the format alone fixes no width to fill; §15.30.3 rule 1 is the standard’s own answer for exactly that shape — an alphanumeric function whose length is “based on its contents” (rule 2) returns “one alphanumeric space character” when the contents are absent — and this determination adopts it. **The clause is a RULE, not a closed list:** the class follows from what determines the returned LENGTH — the rejected argument (zero length), the function (spaces, that many positions), or anything that outlives the rejection (spaces, one position). The two text classes are implemented by exactly two mechanisms, `ExceptionState.ArgumentErrorZeroLength` and `ExceptionState.ArgumentErrorSpaces`, over the one pair of class values in `CobolNet.Runtime.Exceptions.ArgumentSubstitute` (the numeric twin being `ArgumentError`’s own `return 0`); **a guard never spells its own substitute**, and `ArgumentSubstituteDriftTests` keeps that true over `src/Cobol.Net.Runtime/Intrinsics`. ⚠ The `FORMATTED-DATE` / `FORMATTED-DATETIME` / `FORMATTED-TIME` entries above are recorded in the zero-length class as they have always behaved; whether a rejected argument-2 belongs there when the format argument-1 survives is the open question of `kb/Work/PB471`, and it is not silently re-decided here. The exception condition is always SET, so a declarative sees it whenever checking is on; only the substituted result is defined here. ⚠ **This is not the item-93 rule** — a returned value that exceeds the implementor length limit is a separate obligation with its own answer. | `conformance:2023/pb65_domain_guards`; `conformance:2023/l1_argument_default_boolean_length`; `conformance:2002/pb59_char_representative`; `conformance:2002/l1_char_national_argument_bound`; `conformance:2002/l1_char_national_also_positions`; `conformance:2014/pb470_locale_argument_substitute`; `conformance:2002/pb470_locale_argument_substitute_2002`; `unit:ArgumentSubstituteDriftTests.NoIntrinsicGuard_SpellsItsOwnSubstitutedTextResult` |
| DOC-A.1-195 | **SYNCHRONIZED clause — effect on elementary items and containing records or groups; implicit filler generation**, §13.18.55.4 GR9 · §8.5.1.6.3, required + documented | **COBOL.NET performs NO physical alignment for SYNCHRONIZED and generates NO implicit FILLER for it.** The clause is parsed, recorded on the data item and edition-gated (SYNCHRONIZED on a GROUP item is a 2023 introduction — COBOLNET0900 below it, `negative/sync_group_below_2023`), and it changes no offset and no width. Against GR9’s own list: **a)** the external-media format of a record or group containing a SYNCHRONIZED elementary item is byte-identical to the same record written without the clause; **b)** no FILLER is generated when “the elementary item immediately preceding an item containing the SYNCHRONIZED clause does not terminate at an appropriate natural boundary”, so GR9 b)1. (the size of the containing group) and b)2. (the bytes allocated when that group is the object of a REDEFINES clause) never differ from the unsynchronized layout either — `PIC X(1)` followed by `PIC 9(4) COMP SYNCHRONIZED` occupies **3** bytes, not 4, and GR8 b)’s per-occurrence FILLER is vacuous inside a table. GR10’s *optional* automatic alignment is **not specified**, so nothing is aligned without the clause either; and §8.5.1.6.3’s implementor-defined filler for **a bit data item described with the SYNCHRONIZED clause** is likewise **zero bit positions**, the bit item sitting exactly where the same item without the clause would (`BitLayout.ExtentBits` never reads the flag — one layout law, so the clause cannot produce a second answer). GR11 adds no file-level synchronization. **Why:** the typed-native model has no byte substrate to pad — a record IS a `record struct` — and a determination of “no padding” keeps `FUNCTION BYTE-LENGTH`, the group image, the record written to a file, a SORT key and a REDEFINES view one number. BOTH halves are measured, so neither is a documented-but-unmeasured claim: the golden’s **`SYNC=3`** leg (an implementation aligning the binary item to a 2-byte boundary answers 4) measures GR9 b), and its **`SBIT=1`** leg (two same-level 3-bit `USAGE BIT` siblings, only the second SYNCHRONIZED — an implementation generating §8.5.1.6.3 source-(c) filler to byte-align it answers 2) measures the bit-item half. | `conformance:2002/l1_byte_length_implicit_filler` |
| DOC-A.1-204 | **TURN directive — whether location information is available when the LOCATION phrase is not specified**, §7.3.25.4 GR7, required + documented | §7.3.25.4 GR7: “If the LOCATION phrase is specified, all information necessary to identify a source statement for the EXCEPTION-LOCATION, EXCEPTION-LOCATION-N, and EXCEPTION-STATEMENT functions is made available to the run unit. If the LOCATION phrase is not specified, the implementor shall specify whether this information is made available or not.” **COBOL.NET does NOT make it available: no location information is saved unless LOCATION is written.** The determination is uniform across both mechanisms whose LOCATION option GR7 governs — the explicit `>>TURN … CHECKING ON` directive and the *implicit* TURN directive §14.9.28.4 GR14 supplies for a PERFORM WHEN phrase (“If LOCATION is specified, that implicit TURN directive contains LOCATION”) — so a program cannot get location data through one route that the other withholds. The consequence is the no-location arm of §15.30.3 r1 / §15.31.3 r1 / §15.32.3 r1 on **every** unlocated condition: `FUNCTION EXCEPTION-LOCATION` returns one alphanumeric space, `FUNCTION EXCEPTION-LOCATION-N` one national space, and `FUNCTION EXCEPTION-STATEMENT` 63 spaces. **This is a determination, not a limitation:** with LOCATION written the full three-part location string IS produced (§3 carries its part-3 determination), and the choice keeps a run unit from paying to record source positions it never asked for. Both legs are measured side by side, the TURN one and the PERFORM one, because GR14’s implicit directive is the leg no earlier golden used. | `conformance:2023/l1_exception_no_location_arms`; `conformance:2023/exception_statement_ambient` |
| DOC-A.1-163 | **REPOSITORY paragraph — when the AS phrase is required**, §12.3.8.4 GR2, required + documented | **The AS phrase is never required.** Every specifier that prints `[ AS literal-n ]` — class (literal-1), interface (literal-2), program (literal-3), property (literal-4) and user-defined function (literal-5), §12.3.8.2 — accepts it and resolves without it. **With AS**, the declared word is a local name for the definition whose EXTERNALIZED name is the literal (§12.3.8.4 GR2; GR10 NOTE 1 / GR11 NOTE 2 for programs and functions): the class or interface whose CLASS-ID / INTERFACE-ID `AS literal-1` (or name) equals it (`OoRepositoryScope` → `OoClassTable.FindByExternalizedName`), the function whose FUNCTION-ID externalized name equals it (`BinderDriver.UserFunctionsOf`), the program likewise (`BinderDriver.ProgramPrototypesOf`), and for a property the class property of that name (§12.3.8.3 SR16 a); `ReferenceResolver.OoTryBindPropertyReference`). **Without AS**, a program specifier's externalized name is the declared word (GR10 NOTE 1); a class, interface or function specifier names the compilation group's definition DECLARED with that word — GR6 / GR9 leave "how the information in the class specifier and the external repository are used to determine which class is used" to the implementor, and matching the declared word keeps a same-word specifier working whatever AS literal the definition itself carries. A literal §12.3.8.3 SR2 refuses (not alphanumeric or national, figurative, or zero-length) is COBOLNET1761, after which a class, interface, property or function specifier resolves by its declared word and a program specifier registers no prototype; SR1's inconsistent repeat is COBOLNET1761 too — the whole specification compared (kind, AS literal, EXPANDS phrase), keyed on the name across every specifier kind (`DataBinder.CheckRepositorySpecification`; kb/Work PB974, PB1017). | `conformance:2002/pb974_repository_as_phrase`; `conformance:negative/pb974-repository-class-as-zero-length` |
| DOC-A.1-165 | **RETRY phrase — interval between attempts to obtain access to a locked file or record**, §14.7.9.3 GR1, required + documented | **The interval is ZERO: attempts are made back to back, with no wait between them, and the phrase never sleeps.** GR1 leaves the interval between the `n TIMES` re-attempts to the implementor. The ground is structural rather than a convenience: every file and record lock COBOL.NET arbitrates is held by a **file connector of the executing run unit** (§9.1.13.9 / §9.1.13.8 are defined over "another file connector", and two SELECTs bound to one resolved host path are two connectors over one physical file), and a connector cannot release a lock while another statement of the same run unit is executing. No releaser can therefore run *between* two attempts, so a nonzero interval could only delay an identical answer — and a blocking wait would hang the program on a guaranteed failure. `RETRY 2 TIMES` accordingly makes two immediate re-attempts and then lands the conflict's own §9.1.13 status. ⚠ Do not "fix" this into a `Thread.Sleep`. Implemented at `FileRegistry.RetryLoop`; the design rationale is `docs/COBOLNET_FILES_DESIGN.md` **D8**. | `unit:CobolFileLockTests.RetryLoop_AttemptCount_FollowsGR1AndGR4` |
| DOC-A.1-166 | **RETRY phrase — maximum meaningful time-out value and internal representation; technique for determining frequency of retries**, §14.7.9.3 GR2, required + documented | **Internal representation: `9(1)V9(0)` — n = 1, m = 0** (GR2 requires m, which may be zero, and n, which shall be greater than zero). **Maximum meaningful value of arithmetic-expression-2: 0.** **Frequency of retries: none are made during the timeout period,** which is the only technique consistent with a zero-length period. The ground is item 165's: no lock releaser exists between attempts inside one run unit, so no positive timeout period can change the outcome. GR2 clamps every SECONDS amount into the zero-length period ("if arithmetic-expression-2 is greater than this maximum meaningful value, the maximum meaningful value is placed into the temporary data item"), GR4b's "attempts as specified in General rule 2" performs none, and §14.7.9.3's closing paragraph lands the conflict's own §9.1.13 status — **the same answer GR4a gives for a zero or negative expression**. `RETRY FOR 0 SECONDS` and `RETRY FOR 30 SECONDS` therefore agree, by one rule rather than by a special case; that agreement is the observable form of this determination and is pinned by `2023/delete_file_sharing` (`DELSC0` / `DELSC30`). The amount is still evaluated and passed, because it is GR4a's screen input. Note that GR2's temporary is an implicit COMPUTE **without** the ROUNDED phrase — truncation at m — which is a *different* rule from GR1's round-up on the TIMES count; the two arms keep separate renderings in `SequentialIoEmitter.RenderRetry`. | `conformance:2023/delete_file_sharing`; `unit:CobolFileLockTests.RetryLoop_AttemptCount_FollowsGR1AndGR4` |
| DOC-A.1-109 | **I-O status 52 — conditions under which deadlock is detected**, §9.1.13.8 item 2, required + documented | **A deadlock is detected in exactly one circumstance: a `RETRY FOREVER` phrase waiting on a record that is currently locked by another file connector** (the §9.1.13.8 item 1 record operation conflict). §14.7.9.3 GR3 requires FOREVER to "attempt to gain access to a locked resource until the input-output operation has been completed"; the holder of that lock is a connector of the executing run unit and cannot release while this statement runs, so the operation can never complete and the wait is genuinely unterminating — which is what makes it a deadlock rather than an expired timeout. Every other exhausted retry lands the conflict's own status: `None`/`n TIMES`/`FOR n SECONDS` on a record conflict answer '51', and **no file sharing conflict ever answers '52'**, because §9.1.13.9 defines only '61' (OPEN) and '62' (DELETE FILE) and no deadlock value at all. ⛔ That asymmetry is deliberate — §14.9.10.4 GR15b is imperative ("*The* value … is placed") where the record-conflict twin GR6b says only "*A* value" — and answering '52' for a file conflict would additionally raise the wrong exception-name, since §9.1.13.1 keys it on the first digit ('5' → EC-I-O-RECORD-OPERATION, '6' → EC-I-O-FILE-SHARING). Decided in ONE place, `FileRegistry.ExhaustionStatus`; the design is `docs/COBOLNET_FILES_DESIGN.md` **D8**; pinned cell by cell by `CobolFileLockTests.RetryLoop_LandsTheConflictsOwnStatus_ByClass` and end to end by `2002/file_sharing_seq` (`READD5=52`), `2002/file_sharing_mutate` (`REWQFV=52`), `2002/pb142_retry_open_sharing` and `2023/pb142_retry_conflict_class_ec`. | `unit:CobolFileLockTests.RetryLoop_LandsTheConflictsOwnStatus_ByClass`; `conformance:2002/file_sharing_seq`; `conformance:2002/file_sharing_mutate`; `conformance:2002/pb142_retry_open_sharing`; `conformance:2023/pb142_retry_conflict_class_ec` |
| DOC-A.1-110 | **I-O status 9x — the value of x**, §9.1.13.11 item 1, optional + documented when provided | **COBOL.NET defines exactly ONE implementor-defined I-O status: `'90'`, the LINAGE value-rule violation of §13.18.34.4 GR6 b)** — the page size is not greater than zero (GR6 b) 1), or a specified footing start is not greater than zero or is greater than the page size (GR6 b) 2). **Why the condition needs a status at all.** GR6 b) 2 says only that *"the EC-I-O-LINAGE exception condition is set to exist"*, and Table 13 makes EC-I-O-LINAGE **Fatal**; §14.6.13.1.3 3) then routes it — *"If the exception condition is a fatal EC-I-O exception condition, then the rules for 9.1.13, I-O status apply"* — through rules that are entirely about the I-O status, and §9.1.13.1 lists the WRITE among the statements whose execution sets one. Without a status the whole §9.1.12 / §14.9.49.4 GR6 path is closed and the condition is observable only to a program that writes a COBOL-2002 `>>TURN`, which would leave a COBOL-85 program no way to see it at all. **Why 9x.** §9.1.13.2–§9.1.13.10 enumerate every other class's values and none of them is this condition; §9.1.13.11 is the slot the standard provides — *"An implementor-defined condition exists. This condition shall not duplicate any other condition specified by another I-O status value"* — and a `'9'` first digit is FATAL under this implementation's own determination (§9.1.13.1: *"any that begin with the digit 9 that the implementor defines as fatal"*), which is what Table 13's Fatal requires. A value in any other class would assert a condition class the standard defines as something else (a `'5x'` says the record is locked by another file connector; each `'4x'` is one of nine enumerated logic errors), so the surveyed GnuCOBOL value `'57'` is NOT followed here: it is in a nonfatal class, and its first digit raises EC-I-O-RECORD-OPERATION under §9.1.13.1 — the wrong condition, in the wrong fatality. **⛔ THE STATUS DOES NOT CARRY THE CONDITION ALONE.** §9.1.13.1's status→EC correspondence reads a `'9x'` as EC-I-O-IMP, and GR6 b) 2 names EC-I-O-LINAGE, which appears nowhere in that correspondence; §14.6.13.1.1's *"Unless otherwise specified, if more than one exception is detected during the execution of a statement, the one that is set to exist is undefined"* is what lets the specific rule win. The connector therefore records the NAME beside the status (`FileConnector.IoConditionName`, cleared by the one `Status` setter so it cannot outlive its operation) and `FileRegistry.IoConditionName` is the ONE place the named condition and the correspondence are combined — the generated `__IoCheckEc` hook reads it and never applies the correspondence itself. **What the status means for the program.** The operation is unsuccessful, so a format-1 `USE AFTER STANDARD ERROR PROCEDURE` declarative fires (§14.9.49.4 GR6) and the FILE STATUS item reads `90`; the file connector STAYS OPEN, because GR6 b) 2's *"remains at that value until the file is closed"* and *"all subsequent WRITE statements referencing the file"* both presuppose it; LINAGE-COUNTER is 0 until the CLOSE and every later WRITE re-reports the same status without reaching the medium or releasing a record (§14.9.51.4 GR12). With checking for EC-I-O-LINAGE not enabled, §14.6.13.1.3 8) hands the continuation to the implementor and the answer is the one GR6 b) 2 itself writes down — execution continues with the statement following. With checking enabled the standard fatal protocol applies unchanged (§14.6.13.1.3 4)–7), through the existing `__IoCheckEc`); **that general fatal-status policy is A.1 item 103 / item 218 and is NOT settled here** — it is kb/Work PB322 determination E. Raised at `SequentialConnector.EvaluateLinage` (the ONE GR6 b) evaluator, reached from the OPEN OUTPUT completion and from both page transitions) via `FileStatusCode.LinageValueViolation`. | `conformance:2023/pb526_linage_ec_condition`; `conformance:85/pb526_linage_ec_condition_85`; `conformance:2023/pb526_linage_ec_checking`; `conformance-test:LinageConformanceTests.Gr6b1_PageSizeZeroAtOpenOutput_IsTheLinageStatus_AndExecutionContinues`; `conformance-test:LinageConformanceTests.Gr6b2_ViolationAtOverflow_LatchesUntilClose_AndTheCloseReleasesIt` |
| DOC-A.1-24 | **CLOSE statement (closing operations)**, §14.9.6.4 GR3 symbol c, required + documented | **Every physical file COBOL.NET supports is §14.9.6.4 GR2 category (a) Non-unit or category (d) Non-sequential single/multi-unit — never (b) or (c).** A sequential file is (a): a connector resolves ONE host path on mass storage and there is no reel, unit or volume behind it. That placement is FORCED rather than chosen — §9.1.13.2 item 6 defines the `'07'` this compiler reports for the NO REWIND, REEL/UNIT and FOR REMOVAL phrases as the status of a CLOSE that *“references a physical file on a non-reel/unit medium”*, and Table 14 prints symbol g only in the Non-unit column. A relative or indexed file is (d) by its organization alone. **The closing operations symbol c names are:** flush every buffered record to the host file, persist the keyed or relative store, release the OS handle, take the connector out of its open mode (§14.9.6.4 GR8) and release the file lock and every record lock the connector holds (§14.9.6.4 GR9 / §9.1.16). An OS-level failure of any of them is the §9.1.13.6 item 1 permanent error `'30'`, and the connector is dissociated and out of its open mode either way. **The record area after a CLOSE:** §14.9.6.4 GR7 makes it unavailable once no file-name of its SAME RECORD AREA group is open, and the standard defines nothing about referencing an unavailable area — COBOL.NET's realization is that **the storage keeps its last content** for the life of the runtime element (it is a typed field of the emitted class), which is also what every surveyed implementation does; the same answer covers Annex A.2 item 5's undefined unsuccessful-CLOSE case. **Consequences of the (a)/(d) placement:** Table 14's Sequential single-unit and Sequential multi-unit columns are unreachable, so symbols a, b, d and f (previous units, no rewind of the current reel, unit removal, rewind) are never owed and symbol e's two unit-media branches are vacuous; `CLOSE … REEL/UNIT [FOR REMOVAL]` is symbol e's non-unit branch — successful, the file stays OPEN, the file position indicator is unchanged, `'07'`, nothing else — and `CLOSE … WITH NO REWIND` is c,g. The placement lives in code as `PhysicalFileCategory` on `FileConnector`, so a future unit-structured medium must pick a category and Table 14 routes it (kb/Work PB235). | `unit:CloseTable14Tests.Category_PlacesEverySupportedConnectorKind`; `conformance:85/pb141_close_forms`; `conformance:85/pb235_same_record_area_close`; `conformance:2002/pb235_close_unit_locks` |
| DOC-A.1-6 | **Alignment of alphanumeric group items (and strongly-typed group items) relative to the first item within the group**, §8.5.1.6.1 · §8.5.1.6.5, required + documented | **Always coincident.** Every alphanumeric group item, and every strongly-typed group item (a group declared by a `TYPEDEF STRONG` type, 2002 onward), starts at the same byte as the first item subordinate to it, whatever that item's class or usage: COBOL.NET never places slack bytes ahead of a group's first item, and none between items either (no automatic alignment, item 7; no alignment or implicit FILLER for SYNCHRONIZED, item 195) — the only implicit filler is the filler bits §8.5.1.6.3 itself requires after a bit data item to reach the next byte (`05 B1 PIC 1(3) USAGE BIT` followed by `05 B2 PIC X` is a 2-byte group). A group of byte-sized items is therefore exactly the sum of its subordinate items' sizes: `05 G1 PIC 9(4) COMP` followed by `05 G2 PIC X` is a 3-byte group whose first two bytes are G1, and a strongly-typed group of `9(4) COMP-5` then `X` is likewise 3 bytes. The standard's own exceptions (the group coincident with a first item that is national, boolean, message-tag, object or pointer; the type's essential characteristics preserved) hold because there is no other case. The same answer at every edition; one layout authority computes every offset and size (`RecordLayout.OffsetOf`, `RecordLayout.PhysicalWidth`). | `conformance:85/l1c01_group_start_coincident`; `conformance:2002/l1c01_group_start_typed_national_bit` |
| DOC-A.1-7 | **Alignment of data for increased object-code efficiency (special or automatic alignment, implicit filler, semantics of statements)**, §8.5.1.6.4 · §13.18.55.4 GR10, optional (documented if provided) | **Not provided.** COBOL.NET provides no special type of alignment and specifies no automatic alignment: every item sits immediately after its predecessor with no implicit FILLER (other than the filler bits §8.5.1.6.3 requires after a bit data item, which is not alignment for efficiency), with or without the SYNCHRONIZED clause (whose own effect is item 195), so no statement sees a group differently because of alignment — `05 PIC X`, `05 PIC S9(9) COMP-5`, `05 PIC X`, `05 COMP-2` is a 14-byte group at every edition (`RecordLayout.PhysicalWidth`). | — |
| DOC-A.1-8 | **ALPHABET clause — ordinal number of characters in the native coded character sets**, §12.3.7.4 GR6, required + documented | **Code-unit order: the ordinal position of a native character is its UTF-16 code unit plus one**, in BOTH native sets. The native alphanumeric and the native national coded character sets are each the 65,536 UTF-16 code units, one per character position (item 33), ordered by code-unit value: X"00" (U+0000) is ordinal 1, `"A"` is 66, X"FF" is 256, `"€"` (U+20AC) is 8,365, and U+FFFF is 65,536; the national set uses the same numbering (`N"A"` is 66). These are the ordinals FUNCTION ORD returns and FUNCTION CHAR / CHAR-NATIONAL accept whenever no ALPHABET names a different program collating sequence (`CobolIntrinsics.Ord`, `CobolIntrinsics.Char`, `CobolIntrinsics.CharNational`); an ordinal outside 1–65,536 sets EC-ARGUMENT-FUNCTION. The same numbering at every edition (the national set exists from 2002). ⚠ HIGH-VALUE and the figurative extremes are still computed over U+0000–U+00FF rather than the whole native set (kb/Work PB1093, open). | `conformance:85/l1c01_native_ordinal_positions`; `conformance:2002/l1c01_native_ordinal_positions_wide` |
| DOC-A.1-9 | **Alphanumeric literals — number of hexadecimal digits that map to an alphanumeric character**, §8.3.3.2.3 SR6, required + documented | **Two.** Each pair of hexadecimal digits in a hexadecimal alphanumeric literal (`X"…"`, §8.3.3.2.2 format 2) is ONE alphanumeric character whose code is the pair's value: `X"41424344"` is `"ABCD"`, `X"4a4B"` is `"JK"` (either case of A–F), and `FUNCTION LENGTH(X"414243")` is 3. A literal whose digit count is not a whole number of pairs violates §8.3.3.2.3 SR6 and is rejected with **COBOLNET1635** (`X"414"`, `X"4"`), in a VALUE clause and in a procedure statement alike (`CobolLiteral.HexGroupViolation`, decoded by `CobolLiteral.DecodeHex`). The national twin, four digits per national character, is item 122. ⚠ kb/Work PB1393 (open): the COBOLNET1635 check is not yet applied to a hexadecimal literal written as an operand of a concatenation expression (`"Z" & X"4"`) or inside a keyword-omitted intrinsic argument. | `conformance:negative/r03-hex-literal-digit-grouping` |
| DOC-A.1-12 | **Byte — number of bits in**, §8.1.2, required + documented | **Eight**, on every supported computer (COBOL.NET's one target is the .NET runtime, whose byte is 8 bits). Every byte count the compiler reports or lays out rests on it: a `USAGE BIT` item of `PIC 1(8)` occupies exactly one byte and one of `PIC 1(9)` or `PIC 1(12)` occupies two (a partial trailing byte counts whole), `FUNCTION BYTE-LENGTH` counts 8-bit bytes (`PIC X(4)` is 4, `PIC 9(4) COMP` is 2, `PIC N(2)` is 4), and a record written to a file is a sequence of 8-bit bytes. The bit-to-byte law is `BitLayout.BitsPerCharacter` (= 8) through `BitLayout.Characters`. The same answer at every edition. | `conformance:2002/l1_byte_length_implicit_filler` |
| DOC-A.1-16 | **CALL statement — calling a non-COBOL program**, §14.9.4.4 GR3g, required + documented | **Not possible: COBOL.NET executes no non-COBOL program through CALL.** A CALL resolves only to a COBOL.NET program — one in the run unit, or a separately compiled COBOL.NET module `<program-name>.dll` in the application directory that carries the COBOL.NET module registrar (`ProgramTable.ProbeSiblingModule`). Every other target, including a .NET assembly that is present in the application directory (`CALL "Antlr4.Runtime.Standard"`) or a system library name held in an identifier, is **not located**, so GR3g's non-COBOL branch is never reached: the CALL is unsuccessful with **EC-PROGRAM-NOT-FOUND** (GR3b) and continues per GR3h — the ON EXCEPTION imperative statement when one is written (ON OVERFLOW before 2023); otherwise, with checking for EC-PROGRAM-NOT-FOUND enabled, the applicable exception declarative (item 17), and with checking not enabled (and always at 85) the run unit terminates abnormally naming EC-PROGRAM-NOT-FOUND. There is no CALL-CONVENTION directive (§7.3.9), so a program cannot be declared non-COBOL either (item 19); program-name formation and locating for non-COBOL programs (items 13 and 15) therefore never arise. The same at every edition. | — |
| DOC-A.1-17 | **CALL statement — other effects of an unsuccessful CALL whose exception condition is handled by exception processing**, §14.9.4.4 GR3h item 2, required + documented | **None beyond the transfer of control.** GR3h item 2 applies when the called program was not successfully called (EC-PROGRAM-NOT-FOUND, -PTR-NULL, -RESOURCES, -ARG-MISMATCH, -RECURSIVE-CALL or an EC-EXTERNAL condition), checking for that condition is enabled, and no ON EXCEPTION phrase is written (a NOT ON EXCEPTION phrase alone does not count). The applicable declarative runs; if it returns control with `RESUME AT NEXT STATEMENT`, execution continues after the end of the CALL statement, and the CALL has had no other effect: **no program is activated or placed in its initial state, every USING argument and the RETURNING item keep the values they held before the CALL** (`CALL "NOSUCHPG" USING X RETURNING R` leaves `X` = `"ABCD"` and `R` = 1234), NOT ON EXCEPTION is not executed, and `FUNCTION EXCEPTION-STATUS` names the condition until the next one. A declarative that ends without RESUME has no next executable statement for a fatal condition (§14.6.3), so the run unit terminates abnormally (§14.6.12). The routing is `CallEmitter`'s exception partition (kb/Work PB233). 2002 onward (the exception-condition model). | — |
| DOC-A.1-21 | **CHAR function — which one of the multiple characters is returned**, §15.15.4 r2 (second sentence), required + documented | The alphanumeric twin of item 22. §15.15.4 r2 has two branches and only the second is an implementor obligation. **Branch 1 — the order is DEFINED** (an `ALPHABET` literal phrase with `ALSO`): the standard answers, and COBOL.NET returns the first character defined for the position — under `ALPHABET AL IS "C" ALSO "A" ALSO "B" "D"` as the program collating sequence, `FUNCTION CHAR(FUNCTION ORD("A"))` is `"C"` (`LiteralPhraseCollation.CharAt`). **Branch 2 — the order is UNDEFINED, and this is item 21:** an `ALPHABET … IS LOCALE` sequence is an algorithm, not a written order, so every set of characters the locale collates equally shares one ordinal position with no order among its members. **COBOL.NET returns the LOWEST-CODED member of that rank** (`LocaleCollation.CharAt` reading `OrderVector.FirstOfRank`, materialized once per collator), so the answer is the same for every invocation at a given implementation, collating sequence and ordinal position: X"00", X"01" and X"1F" all share ordinal 1 of a locale sequence, and `FUNCTION CHAR(1)` is X"00". 2002 onward (the LOCALE phrase). | `conformance:2002/l1c02_char_locale_tie` |
| DOC-A.1-26 | **COBOL character repertoire — more than one encoding in a compilation group, and the control functions that switch between them**, §8.1.3.2 GR2, conditionally required (provided) + documented | **Provided, per FILE, by byte-order mark; there are no control functions.** Every file of a compilation group — the source file named on the command line and each library text a COPY statement brings in — is decoded independently, so one compilation group may mix encodings file by file. A file that begins with a byte-order mark is read in the encoding the mark names: UTF-8 (EF BB BF), UTF-16 little- or big-endian (FF FE / FE FF), or UTF-32; a file with no byte-order mark is read as UTF-8. The mark itself is not source text. The encoding cannot change inside a file: no in-stream control function or directive switches it, and none is part of the syntax. A UTF-8 main program that COPYs a UTF-16LE library text holding `VALUE "O€K"` compiles and displays `O€K`. Both source text and library text are read by `CompilationInputs.ReadAllText`. The same at every edition. | — |
| DOC-A.1-29 | **Compile-time arithmetic — mode of arithmetic used and rules for intermediate data handling**, §7.3.6 (§7.3.6.3 GR2 · §7.3.6.2 SR2), required + documented | **A mode unique to compile-time arithmetic: .NET `System.Decimal`**, the same for a constant entry (`CONSTANT AS`), a `>>DEFINE` value and a `>>IF`/`>>EVALUATE` operand (one evaluator, `CompileTimeExpressionEvaluator`). It is neither a §8.8.1 standard mode nor the runtime's native arithmetic. **Precision and range:** each intermediate result is a decimal number of up to 28–29 significant digits (at most 28 of them after the decimal point) whose magnitude is below about 7.9×10²⁸; an intermediate result outside that range is a compile-time error — COBOLNET1547 in a constant entry, COBOLNET1619 in a directive — never a wrapped or truncated value (`9999999999999999999999999999 * 10` is refused). **Intermediate rounding:** addition, subtraction, multiplication and division are exact whenever the exact result fits those digits; otherwise the result is **rounded to the nearest representable value, a tie to the even digit** — division does NOT truncate: `2 / 3 * 3` is 2 and `100 / 7 * 7` is 100 (a truncating division would give 1 and 99), while `1 / 3 * 3` is 0; `0.0000000000000000000000000005 / 2 * 10000000000000000000000000000` is 2 and `0.0000000000000000000000000015 / 2 * 10000000000000000000000000000` is 8. **Final result:** truncated toward zero to its integer part, as §7.3.6.3 GR3 requires (`-7 / 2` is -3, `2.5 + 0` is 2). The exponentiation operator (§7.3.6.2 SR1a) and a division by zero (SR1c) are rejected. 2002 onward. | — |
| DOC-A.1-32 | **Computer's coded character set — coded character values for certain COBOL items**, §8.1.2, required + documented | **Fixed at compile time; the same UTF-16 code unit in the alphanumeric and the national set** (the two native sets share one code-unit numbering, item 33), so each element's alphanumeric and national values are identical and therefore equivalent. Figurative constants: **SPACE** U+0020, **QUOTE** U+0022 (the quotation mark), **ZERO** U+0030. **Space filling** of alphanumeric and national items: U+0020 (`MOVE "A" TO` a `PIC X(4)` item stores 41 20 20 20). Editing characters: **B** inserts U+0020 (the same value as SPACE and space fill), **0** U+0030 (the same value as ZERO and the digit 0), digits **0–9** U+0030–U+0039, **C R** U+0043 U+0052, **D B** U+0044 U+0042, **E** U+0045, **-** U+002D, **+** U+002B, **\*** U+002A, **/** U+002F, **,** U+002C, **.** U+002E. The **default currency string** for the `$` symbol is `$` U+0024. None of these values depends on the run-time environment. The figurative values come from `FigurativeConstants.FillChar`. The same at every edition (national data from 2002). | `conformance:85/l1c05_coded_character_values`; `conformance:2002/l1c05_coded_character_values_national` |
| DOC-A.1-35 | **Correspondence between lowercase and uppercase letters when no locale is in effect**, §15.97 UPPER-CASE / §15.57 LOWER-CASE function, returned-value rule 4 (§15.97.4 r4, §15.57.4 r4), required + need not be documented (VOLUNTARY — kept because the mapping is observable in every program that folds case) | When neither the LOCALE phrase (r2) nor a CHARACTER CLASSIFICATION (r3) is in effect, UPPER-CASE and LOWER-CASE map through the **.NET invariant-culture simple case tables** — `CobolIntrinsics.UpperCase` is `ToUpperInvariant`, `CobolIntrinsics.LowerCase` is `ToLowerInvariant` — identically in every edition and whatever SET LOCALE, `LANG` or `LC_ALL` says. The correspondence is **one character to one character**, so the returned value always has argument-1's length (r5): `a`→`A`, `é`→`É`, `ſ` (U+017F)→`S`, `A`→`a`, `É`→`é`; `ß` stays `ß` (never `SS`), and the Turkish letters dotless `ı` (U+0131) and dotted `İ` (U+0130) have no invariant correspondence and are returned unchanged, as is every other character without one (r6). The Turkish `i`↔`İ` mapping is available only through a locale (the `LOCALE` phrase or a locale classification — DETERMINATION L9, §3, `CobolLocale`). | `conformance:2002/pb64t5_case_locale_phrase` |
| DOC-A.1-42 | **Cultural ordering table — allowable content of the literal that identifies it**, §12.3.7 SPECIAL-NAMES paragraph, GR17 (§12.3.7.4 GR17 — `ORDER TABLE ordering-name-1 IS literal-9`), conditionally required (the cultural-ordering facility is provided, A.4.9 item 11) + documented | literal-9 (an alphanumeric or national literal, or a concatenation expression of that class, neither zero-length nor a symbolic-character figurative constant — §12.3.7.3 SR10/SR11, COBOLNET0898; ⚠ today every figurative constant, SPACE and ALL literal included, is also refused with COBOLNET0898, which §8.3.3.6.3 SR1 does not permit — an open defect, kb/Work PB1537) may name: **(1)** the ISO/IEC 14651:2020 default table **`ISO 14651_2020_TABLE1`**, matched case-insensitively with spaces and underscores interchangeable and any run of them treated as one (`iso_14651_2020_table1` is the same table) — the CLDR root collation (UCA 17.0.0) in its ISO/IEC 14651-style four-level configuration; or **(2)** as an implementor extension, a **locale tag** (`-` or `_` separators) that this implementation knows: one with a CLDR collation file of its own or of its region-less form, one with a site `.tailor` file, or a culture .NET recognizes. A `-u-co-` collation type is honoured (`de-u-co-phonebk` is German phonebook order); a locale with CLDR data takes its tailored order (`sv_SE` orders `ä` after `b`, where the default table puts it before), and a culture .NET recognizes that no collation data covers collates by the root order. Any other spelling — including another edition's table name such as `ISO 14651_2011_TABLE1` — is legal source that draws the **COBOLNET1662 warning**, and every `FUNCTION STANDARD-COMPARE` reference to that ordering-name sets **EC-ORDER-NOT-SUPPORTED** (§15.85.4 r2); with that checking off the function returns `=`. The compile-time advice and the run-time resolution are one resolver, `CollationEngine.TryGetOrderingTable` (bound in `DataBinder.OrderTableBind`). The clause is COBOL-2002 and later; below 2002 it is COBOLNET0900. | `conformance:2002/pb101_standard_compare`; `conformance:2002/pb101_ec_order_not_supported` |
| DOC-A.1-43 | **Currency symbol — equivalence of a currency symbol that is not a COBOL character**, §12.3.7 SPECIAL-NAMES paragraph, SR20 (the equivalence) as consumed by SR21 (§12.3.7.3 SR20/SR21), required + documented | A currency symbol outside the COBOL character repertoire is equivalent to **exactly the characters that have the same .NET invariant-culture uppercase mapping** (`char.ToUpperInvariant`) and to no other character — simple case pairs, no normalization, no width folding: `é` ≡ `É`, `ω` ≡ `Ω`, full-width `ｅ` ≡ full-width `Ｅ` (but not `E`), while dotless `ı` (U+0131), which has no invariant mapping, is equivalent only to itself. Consequences: **(1)** a PICTURE character-string may write the symbol in either member of its pair — with `CURRENCY SIGN IS "é"`, `PIC É9.99` holding 1.5 edits as `é1.50`, and with `CURRENCY SIGN IS "EUR" WITH PICTURE SYMBOL "é"` as `EUR1.50` (the inserted text is always the clause's currency string as written); **(2)** two CURRENCY clauses whose symbols are equivalent but whose currency strings differ are **COBOLNET0891** (SR21) — `PICTURE SYMBOL "é"` bound to `"EUR"` and `"É"` bound to `"USD"` is refused; **(3)** the one non-COBOL character this mapping makes equivalent to a COBOL letter is `ſ` (U+017F) ≡ `S`, which is therefore a prohibited currency symbol (item 44). The same in every edition that has the clause form (the bare single-character form from COBOL-85, the PICTURE SYMBOL form from COBOL-2002). Implemented as the symbol-table key in `DataBinder.SwitchBindCurrency` and the matching in `PictureAnalyzer.Analyze`. | `conformance:85/l1c06_currency_non_cobol_equivalence_85`; `conformance:2002/l1c06_currency_non_cobol_symbols`; `conformance:negative/l1c06-currency-non-cobol-equivalent-rebound` |
| DOC-A.1-44 | **Currency symbol — non-COBOL characters prohibited as a currency symbol**, §8.1.2 computer's coded character set, required + documented | The compile-time coded character set is UTF-16, so it contains every non-COBOL character. **Exactly one of them is prohibited as a currency symbol: `ſ` (LATIN SMALL LETTER LONG S, U+017F)**, because its invariant uppercase is the picture letter `S` (the item-43 equivalence) and §12.3.7.3 SR22/SR27 exclude `S`; it is refused with **COBOLNET0891**, as the excluded COBOL characters are (the digits 0–9, the letters A B C D E N P R S V X Z in either case, space, and `+ - , . * / ; ( ) " =`). Every other non-COBOL character is allowed — `€`, `£`, `é`, `Ω`, `ω`, full-width letters such as `ｚ`, and full-width digits (only the ASCII digits are excluded) — in the bare single-character form and as a PICTURE SYMBOL alike, in every edition. Implemented in `DataBinder.ValidateCurrencyChar`. | `conformance:2002/l1c06_currency_non_cobol_symbols`; `conformance:negative/l1c06-currency-long-s-prohibited` |
| DOC-A.1-51 | **DELETE FILE statement — what deleting the file does**, §14.9.10 DELETE statement, GR20 (§14.9.10.4 GR20), required + documented | A successful DELETE FILE removes **exactly one host file — the connector's resolved path — with one operating-system delete** (`File.Delete`, an unlink on POSIX hosts), for every organization: a sequential, relative or indexed physical file is one host file, and its fixed file attributes live inside it, so no sidecar, index, lock or catalog file exists to be removed (kb/Work PB802). Nothing is moved to a recycle area and nothing can be recovered by the runtime. Before deleting, the file is checked in this order: **open on this connector** → `41` and nothing is deleted (GR13); the GR18 fixed-attribute match, which never fails because the validated attribute set is empty (item 50); **held open by another file connector** — one of this run unit's, or another run unit's (tested by requesting exclusive access to the file: on Windows every outstanding handle refuses it; on Linux and macOS only a handle holding an advisory lock does, which every COBOL.NET file connector's handle holds, so a POSIX unlink never destroys a file another COBOL.NET run unit has open, while a plain open handle of a non-.NET process does not block the delete) → `62` and the file is not deleted (GR15); **absent** → `05`, a successful completion with no action (GR14), and a bare `ASSIGN USING` connector that has never been associated is treated as absent without touching any file; present but not accessible → `37` (GR16); a medium that refuses deletion (a write-protected volume or read-only mount) → `37` (GR17), a sharing refusal raised by the delete itself → `62`, any other host failure → `30`. On success the status is `00` and a later OPEN INPUT of the file reports `35`. Available from COBOL-2023 (COBOLNET0900 below). Implemented in `FileRegistry.DeleteFile` over `HostFile.Probe` and `HostFile.IsHeldByAnother`; the RETRY phrase's handling of the `62` conflict is item 109's. | `conformance:2023/delete_file`; `conformance:2023/delete_file_absent`; `conformance:2023/delete_file_sharing` |
| DOC-A.1-61 | **Dynamic-capacity table — size, location and time of physical allocation**, §8.5.1.9 dynamic-capacity tables (§8.5.1.9.1), required + need not be documented (VOLUNTARY — kept because it explains the cost of growth) | Each `OCCURS DYNAMIC` table is **one managed array of its own, stored outside the record that contains it** (the record holds a reference to it), so growing a table never moves or resizes its containing group. **Time and size:** the array is allocated when the containing data item is created, with room for the larger of the initial current capacity and 4 occurrences — the initial capacity being the FROM integer (or 0), raised to a table VALUE's capacity — and the initial occurrences are initialized at once. It is **reallocated only when a receiving reference or a SET needs a higher capacity than the array holds** (a sending reference beyond the current capacity never allocates or grows the table), by doubling (from at least 4) until it is large enough, never beyond 1,073,741,823 occurrences; newly allocated occurrences, including every intermediate one, are initialized immediately with the element's initial value (whether or not INITIALIZED is written). **Lowering the capacity** (SET … TO / DOWN BY) releases nothing physically — the slots are kept for reuse, as the §8.5.1.9.4 NOTE permits — and an occurrence re-created by a later increase is initialized afresh, never the value it held before. Implemented in `CobolDynTable` (`GrowTo`, `SetCapacity`). ⚠ An allocation the host cannot satisfy is not yet reported as EC-BOUND-TABLE-LIMIT (kb/Work PB1410, open). | — |
| DOC-A.1-65 | **EXIT PROGRAM and GOBACK — how execution continues in a non-COBOL activating runtime element**, §14.9.18 GOBACK statement, GR1 a) (§14.9.18.4; EXIT PROGRAM reaches the same rule through §14.9.14.4 GR3), conditionally required (a .NET host can activate a COBOL.NET program) + documented | **An ordinary managed return: the host's call returns normally and the host continues at its own call site; no exception condition is raised in the host and no .NET exception is thrown to it.** The only non-COBOL activator a COBOL.NET program can have is a .NET host (a non-COBOL element cannot be the target of a CALL — item 19): the host loads the compiled assembly, calls its public `__CobolModule.Register()` once, and activates a program by name through `ProgramRegistry.CallProgram(name, callerPath, args, returning)`. When that program executes GOBACK, or EXIT PROGRAM, the generated entry (`__Activate`) catches the internal return signal and returns, so the statements after the GOBACK are not executed and the host resumes. The RETURNING item and the RAISING phrase in this case are the §3 determination 'Return to a NON-COBOL activating runtime element' (a staged RAISING condition is never raised, because the host enables no checking). Every edition. | — |
| DOC-A.1-69 | **Fatal exception conditions — which are detected at compile time, and when**, §14.6.13.1.3, optional + need not be documented (VOLUNTARY) | **One fatal exception condition is detected at compile time: EC-ORDER-NOT-SUPPORTED.** When a SPECIAL-NAMES `ORDER TABLE ... IS literal` clause names a cultural ordering table this implementation does not provide, the compiler reports **warning COBOLNET1662** at that clause. A FUNCTION STANDARD-COMPARE that uses the table sets EC-ORDER-NOT-SUPPORTED at run time (§15.85.4). **The compiler always produces executable code**, whether checking is enabled or not. The permission §14.6.13.1.3 gives to omit code for a condition detected at compile time is never used, so detection never becomes a compile error. Nothing else is detected at compile time. A constant subscript outside the OCCURS bounds, a constant reference modification outside the item, a literal zero divisor and a constant intrinsic argument outside its domain all compile without a diagnostic. These are general rules, not syntax rules. With checking enabled the program ends at run time with the fatal condition (EC-BOUND-SUBSCRIPT, EC-BOUND-REF-MOD, EC-SIZE-ZERO-DIVIDE, EC-ARGUMENT-FUNCTION); with checking off it continues. Compile-time errors that name a run-time condition, such as COBOLNET1688 for EC-PROGRAM-ARG-MISMATCH, enforce syntax rules and are not detections under this item. The warning is `DiagnosticCatalog.OrderTableUnresolved`. | — |
| DOC-A.1-74 | **Figurative constant values — representation of ZERO, SPACE and QUOTE**, §8.3.3.6 (§8.3.3.6.4 GR1), required + documented | Each has ONE representation, the same code unit in both coded character sets (both are UTF-16, one code unit per character position — item 33): **SPACE is U+0020, ZERO is U+0030 (the character '0'), QUOTE is U+0022 (the character '"')**. In an alphanumeric item that is the one-byte DISPLAY image 0x20 / 0x30 / 0x22 (item 209); in a national item it is the two-byte code unit 0x0020 / 0x0030 / 0x0022 (two bytes per national position, as item 209's row states). So, with no PROGRAM COLLATING SEQUENCE, `MOVE SPACE` to a `PIC X` item gives `FUNCTION ORD` 33, ZERO 49, QUOTE 35, and the national item gives the same values (ORD reports the position in the program collating sequence, so under `PROGRAM COLLATING SEQUENCE IS` an EBCDIC alphabet it reports 65 for SPACE although the stored character is still U+0020); `SPACE = X"20"`, `QUOTE = X"22"`, `ZERO = X"30"` and `QUOTE = NX"0022"` are all true. Neither a PROGRAM COLLATING SEQUENCE, an ALPHABET clause nor a CODE-SET clause changes these values in storage; those clauses affect comparison order and the file's external code set only. The single source for the fill characters is `FigurativeConstants.FillChar`. (A BOOLEAN receiver is outside this item: ZERO fills it with the boolean character '0', and so does SPACE, determination D-B2 in §3.) | — |
| DOC-A.1-76 | **File sharing — which devices allow concurrent access to a physical file**, §9.1.15, required + documented | **All of them.** Every physical file is a path in the host file system: a regular file, or a device path such as `/dev/null` that the host opens as a file. The device classes `DISK` and `PRINTER` both name such a file (item 71). COBOL.NET treats every physical file as one that allows concurrent access, so it is *shareable* in the sense of §14.9.27.4 GR21 and the SHARING clause and SHARING phrase always apply. A second connector's OPEN of the same file is decided by §9.1.15 Table 19: connectors that both specify `SHARING WITH ALL OTHER` (with `LOCK MODE MANUAL`) can be open on one regular file or on `/dev/null` at the same time, each with status 00, and a `SHARING WITH NO OTHER` OPEN of a file another connector has open fails with 61. `ASSIGN TO PRINTER "f"` and `ASSIGN TO DISK "f"` are the same shareable file. This row covers only which files can be shared. How strongly the file lock excludes other run units depends on the host, and that is open under kb/Work PB833. The mode used when no sharing mode is specified (item 77) is open under kb/Work PB322. Implemented by `FileRegistry.SharedOpenAttempt` and `PhysicalFileTable`, which apply Table 19 to every connector without testing the device. | — |
| DOC-A.1-78 | **Fixed file attributes — whether the ability to share a physical file is a fixed file attribute**, §9.1.6, required + documented | **No.** A physical file records nothing about sharing. The sharing mode belongs to each FILE CONNECTOR: it comes from that connector's SHARING clause, or from the SHARING phrase of the OPEN that opens it (§14.9.27.4 GR21–GR23). It is set when the OPEN succeeds and checked against the connectors that have the same physical file open at that moment (§9.1.15 Table 19). When the connector is closed, its sharing mode is gone. So a file created through a `SHARING WITH NO OTHER` connector can later be opened through two `SHARING WITH ALL OTHER` connectors at once, and both OPENs succeed with 00. While the NO OTHER connector is open, any other OPEN of the file fails with 61, and the NO OTHER connector's own OPEN fails with 61 while other connectors have the file open. This is also why A.1 item 129's validated fixed attributes never include sharing. Implemented by `FileRegistry.RegisterSharing` (the per-connector mode) and `FileRegistry.SharedOpenAttempt` (the check at OPEN). | — |
| DOC-A.1-79 | **FLAG-02 directive — the warning mechanism**, §7.3.14 GR1 (§7.3.14.4 GR1), required + documented | **A compile-time warning, COBOLNET1620, for each flagged construct. The warning does not change the compiled program.** `>>FLAG-02` names ALL, or one or more of its five options (EC-PROGRAM-EXCEPTIONS, I-O-STATUS-07, MOVE-TO-SAME-NAME, RANGE-EXCEPTION-FOR-INDEX, TERMINATE-WITH-VARYING) in any order, followed by ON or OFF. If ON and OFF are both omitted, ON applies. An option is on from the line after the directive until a later FLAG-02 turns it off, or turns ALL off, or the compilation group ends. Every option is off by default. While an option is on, each construct §7.3.14.4 GR4 describes for it is reported as `warning COBOLNET1620: <the construct> (<the 2002-to-2014 change>) — flagged by >>FLAG-02 <option>` at the construct's line and column. For example, with I-O-STATUS-07 on, each `CLOSE ... WITH NO REWIND` or `UNIT` is reported. An option word that is not one of the five, or ALL combined with named options, is refused with COBOLNET1622. The directive is a COBOL-2014 element: at `--std 2002` and below it is refused with COBOLNET0900, and at `--std 2023` it still works but draws the obsolete-element warning COBOLNET0903 (§7.3.14.1 NOTE). Known defects in the open notes, not part of this determination: `>>FLAG-02 ON`, `>>FLAG-02 OFF` and a bare `>>FLAG-02` (the printed format leaves ALL un-underlined, so ALL is the default) are currently refused with COBOLNET1622, so write ALL explicitly, and a repeated option word is silently accepted instead of refused (kb/Work PB1374); the EC-PROGRAM-EXCEPTIONS warnings have no source location and are reported once per program (kb/Work PB1376), and some GR4 construct populations are narrower than the rule (kb/Work PB1375). Implemented by `FlagDirectiveLine.TryParse`, `FlagState` and `FlagConformancePass`; the diagnostic is `DiagnosticCatalog.Flag02Warning`. | `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Compile_Flag02IoStatus07On_EmitsCobolnet1620_OnEachPhraseSpelling`; `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Parse_Flag02_OnIsImplicit_WhenOnOffOmitted`; `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Fold_DefaultsOff_AndIsStickyForwardStrictlyAfterTheDirective` |
| DOC-A.1-80 | **FLAG-14 directive — the warning mechanism**, §7.3.15 GR1 (§7.3.15.4 GR1), required + documented | **A compile-time warning, COBOLNET1621, for each flagged construct. The warning does not change the compiled program.** `>>FLAG-14` names ALL, or one or more of its twelve options (COMPILE-TIME-ARITHMETIC-EXPRESSIONS, EVALUATE, I-O-DECLARATIVE, I-O-STATUS-04, I-O-STATUS-07, NUM-ED-ZERO-FIGCONST, READ-PREVIOUS, REF-MOD-ZERO-LENGTH, VALUE-EDITING, VALUE-FIG-CON-LENGTH, VALUE-ZERO, WRITE-END-OF-PAGE) in any order, followed by ON or OFF. An option is on from the line after the directive until a later FLAG-14 turns it off, or turns ALL off, or the compilation group ends. Every option is off by default. While an option is on, each construct §7.3.15.4 GR4 describes for it is reported as `warning COBOLNET1621: <the construct> (<the 2014-to-2023 change>) — flagged by >>FLAG-14 <option>` at the construct's line and column. For example, `>>FLAG-14 READ-PREVIOUS I-O-STATUS-04 ON` reports each READ PREVIOUS statement and each reference that tests a FILE STATUS item for '04', and `>>FLAG-14 VALUE-ZERO ON` reports a numeric-edited item whose VALUE is ZERO. An option word that is not one of the twelve, or ALL combined with named options, is refused with COBOLNET1622. The directive is COBOL-2023 only: at `--std 2014` and below it is refused with COBOLNET0900. Known defects in the open notes, not part of this determination: an omitted ON/OFF is currently refused with COBOLNET1622 instead of meaning ON, and an omitted option list (`>>FLAG-14 ON`, `>>FLAG-14 OFF`, where ALL is the default) is refused the same way, so write ON and ALL explicitly; a repeated option word is silently accepted instead of refused (kb/Work PB1374); and some GR4 construct populations are narrower than the rule, for example the arithmetic expression of a constant entry is not examined under COMPILE-TIME-ARITHMETIC-EXPRESSIONS (kb/Work PB1375). Implemented by `FlagDirectiveLine.TryParse`, `FlagState` and `FlagConformancePass`; the diagnostic is `DiagnosticCatalog.Flag14Warning`. | `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Compile_Flag14ReadPreviousOn_EmitsCobolnet1621`; `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Compile_Flag14ReadPreviousOff_EmitsNoFlagWarning`; `unit:FlagDirectiveTests.Fold_DefaultsOff_AndIsStickyForwardStrictlyAfterTheDirective` |
| DOC-A.1-94 | **GOBACK statement — constraints on the value in the STATUS phrase**, §14.9.18.3 GR10 (printed as §14.9.18.4 GR10 in this text), required + documented | **No value is refused. The STATUS operand becomes the process exit code**, with the same mapping as STOP (A.1 item 192, §3 'STOP RUN / GOBACK termination status'). The syntax rules still apply: identifier-2 must be an integer data item or a data item of usage display or national (§14.9.18.3 SR6), and a numeric literal-1 must be an integer (SR7). The mapping is: **(1)** a numeric operand's integer value is the exit code (a data item's value truncated toward zero); **(2)** a non-numeric operand (an alphanumeric or national item, a non-numeric literal, a figurative constant) is decoded as an unsigned integer from its digit characters, and a position that is not a digit adds no digit, so `STATUS "007"` exits 7, `STATUS "A1B2"` exits 12 and `STATUS "ABEND"` or `STATUS SPACE` exits 0 (`CobolNum.FromAlphanumeric`); **(3)** the host then keeps what it can report: the value is narrowed to a 32-bit integer and a POSIX host keeps only the low 8 bits, so `STATUS 300` exits 44 and `STATUS -3` exits 253. With no STATUS value, `GOBACK WITH ERROR` exits 1 and `WITH NORMAL` (or no phrase) exits 0; a STATUS value, when present, wins over ERROR or NORMAL. The value reaches the operating system only when the GOBACK ends the run unit, that is, in a main program (§14.9.18.4 GR3); in a called program the GOBACK returns to its caller and the status is not used. The phrase is COBOL-2023 only: at `--std 2014` and below it is refused with COBOLNET0900. Implemented by `CallBinder.DecodeGobackPhrases` and the `RunUnit.ExitStatus` setter. | `conformance-test:StopGobackExitCodeTests.TerminationStatus_SetsProcessExitCode`; `conformance-test:StopGobackExitCodeTests.NonNumericStatusOperand_IsLegalAndInterpretedNumerically` |
| DOC-A.1-95 | **Hexadecimal alphanumeric literals — result when no corresponding character exists**, §8.3.3.2 GR4 (printed as §8.3.3.2.4 GR5 in this text), required + documented | **The case does not arise: every hex-character-sequence has a corresponding character.** A hexadecimal alphanumeric literal takes TWO hexadecimal digits per character (§8.3.3.2.3 SR6; any other count is refused, COBOLNET1635), and the group `hh` is the character whose code is U+00hh, which is also its one-byte DISPLAY image (item 209). All 256 values 00 through FF are characters of the alphanumeric set, so each group is stored as that character, including the control codes 00–1F, 7F and 80–9F, with no diagnostic and no exception condition. Example: `X"80FF0041"` is four characters whose `FUNCTION ORD` values are 129, 256, 1 and 66. What a device shows for a control character on DISPLAY is the device's business, not the literal's. Implemented in `CobolLiteral.DecodeHex`. | — |
| DOC-A.1-97 | **Hexadecimal national literals — result when no corresponding character exists**, §8.3.3.5 GR4 (printed as §8.3.3.5.4 GR5 in this text), required + documented | A hexadecimal national literal takes FOUR hexadecimal digits per character (§8.3.3.5.3 SR5; any other count is refused, COBOLNET1635), and each group is stored **verbatim as that UTF-16 code unit**, the national character representation (item 33). A group that is not a character on its own, such as a surrogate half D800–DFFF or a noncharacter such as FFFE or FFFF, is stored unchanged, as one character position, with no diagnostic and no exception condition. It then behaves as any other national character: `FUNCTION LENGTH(NX"D800")` is 1, `FUNCTION ORD` gives the code plus 1 (55297 for D800, 65536 for FFFF), and comparison uses the code unit's value. Two groups that form a valid surrogate pair stay two character positions. On DISPLAY to a UTF-8 device, an unpaired surrogate is written as U+FFFD. Implemented in `CobolLiteral.DecodeHexGroups`. | — |
| DOC-A.1-100 | **Implementor-defined level-2 exception conditions, EC-level-2-IMP — specification and meaning of each specified level-2 IMP exception condition**, §14.6.13.1.6 Table 13 (§14.6.13.1.1, the '-IMP' suffix type), optional + documented if provided | **Provided: 20 of Table 13's 22 EC-level-2-IMP names are valid exception-names, and each is FATAL.** Table 13's 22 names are EC-ARGUMENT-IMP, EC-BOUND-IMP, EC-CONTINUE-IMP, EC-DATA-IMP, EC-EXTERNAL-IMP, EC-FLOW-IMP, EC-FUNCTION-IMP, EC-I-O-IMP, EC-LOCALE-IMP, EC-MCS-IMP, EC-OO-IMP, EC-ORDER-IMP, EC-OVERFLOW-IMP, EC-PROGRAM-IMP, EC-RAISING-IMP, EC-RANGE-IMP, EC-REPORT-IMP, EC-SCREEN-IMP, EC-SIZE-IMP, EC-SORT-MERGE-IMP, EC-STORAGE-IMP and EC-VALIDATE-IMP. EC-CONTINUE-IMP, EC-EXTERNAL-IMP and EC-MCS-IMP are COBOL-2023 names; below 2023 they are refused with COBOLNET0878. EC-SCREEN-IMP and EC-VALIDATE-IMP belong to the screen and VALIDATE modules, which this implementation does not provide (Annex A.4.2, A.4.14). They are refused wherever they are written, with COBOLNET1707 and COBOLNET1710. The other 20 can be used in `>>TURN`, `RAISE EXCEPTION` and wherever an exception-name is allowed. Table 13 leaves their fatality to the implementor (Imp), and this implementation treats every one as fatal. An unhandled one ends the run unit abnormally, and so does a `RAISE EXCEPTION` of one with checking not enabled. **Meaning: the implementation itself sets only EC-PROGRAM-IMP**, and only when a contained program is activated while the program that contains it has no instance (an internal consistency failure of the program table, reported as 'internal: contained program ... activated while its container is not instantiated'). The implementation never sets any of the others, so a program sees them only when it raises them itself. EC-I-O-IMP is Table 13's 'I-O status 9x' condition, but the only 9x status COBOL.NET defines, '90', carries EC-I-O-LINAGE instead (item 110). EC-RAISING-IMP is never set at run time either. The names and fatality are `ExceptionCatalog` (EcFatality.Imp); the one raise site is `ProgramTable.ParentInstance`, through the default exception-name of `CobolCallException`. | — |
| DOC-A.1-101 | **INVOKE statement — behavior when invoking a non-COBOL method**, §14.9.23.4 GR2 b) (and the GR7 e) execution of a non-COBOL method), required + documented | **A non-COBOL method is never invoked: COBOL.NET provides no way to designate one, so every INVOKE reaches a method written in COBOL or fails.** Every method an INVOKE can reach is declared in a COBOL class definition of the compilation group.
